People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

And then I kind of freeze on simple decisions I used to handle without thinking. I’m not sure if that makes sense.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you normally like to do?\n\nPatient: Not as much, no. I mean, I still try, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but… my guitar’s just been sitting in the corner, and I keep backing out of soccer and the shelter shifts. Even cooking or going for a hike, which usually helps, feels kind of like I’m just doing it because I should, not because I actually want to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about the future?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not hopeless, exactly. I mean, I get discouraged, but I’m not sitting there thinking there’s no future or anything. Maybe I just feel a bit unsure about things right now. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s been about a month or so. It kind of started after the clinic lost some staff and my schedule shifted more into late-evening coverage, plus a lot more crisis paperwork. I don’t know if that’s, like, the reason exactly, but that’s when I started noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is present most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to measure that. It’s… there a lot, I guess, but I can still have moments where I’m distracted or things feel kind of normal. So I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say every minute of the day. But yeah, it does seem to be hanging around more often than I’d like. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns recently?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, a little, I guess. Mostly appetite. I don’t really feel hungry most days unless someone kind of reminds me to eat, and then I realize I haven’t had much. I don’t mean to skip meals, it just sort of happens. My pants have been feeling looser too, so… I guess it’s probably noticeable. Is that the kind of change you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is significantly interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’ve been trying to handle it and not make it a big thing, but it got to the point where I told my supervisor I needed help. I was making mistakes and hesitating with clients, and I started worrying it could affect their care.\n\nI don’t want to sound dramatic, but I didn’t really trust myself to keep working safely without getting evaluated. Is that… is that the kind of interference you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your low mood as mild, or is it more pronounced?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it mild, exactly. I mean, I’m still getting through things, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is… but it feels more than just “a little down.” It’s been pretty noticeable and harder to shake off than I’d like. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about the future?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not hopeless, exactly. I mean, I get discouraged, but I’m not sitting there thinking there’s no future or anything. Maybe I just feel a bit unsure about things right now. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s been about a month or so. It kind of started after the clinic lost some staff and my schedule shifted more into late-evening coverage, plus a lot more crisis paperwork. I don’t know if that’s, like, the reason exactly, but that’s when I started noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is present most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to measure that. It’s… there a lot, I guess, but I can still have moments where I’m distracted or things feel kind of normal. So I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say every minute of the day. But yeah, it does seem to be hanging around more often than I’d like. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns recently?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, a little, I guess. Mostly appetite. I don’t really feel hungry most days unless someone kind of reminds me to eat, and then I realize I haven’t had much. I don’t mean to skip meals, it just sort of happens. My pants have been feeling looser too, so… I guess it’s probably noticeable. Is that the kind of change you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is significantly interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’ve been trying to handle it and not make it a big thing, but it got to the point where I told my supervisor I needed help. I was making mistakes and hesitating with clients, and I started worrying it could affect their care.\n\nI don’t want to sound dramatic, but I didn’t really trust myself to keep working safely without getting evaluated. Is that… is that the kind of interference you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your low mood as mild, or is it more pronounced?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it mild, exactly. I mean, I’m still getting through things, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is… but it feels more than just “a little down.” It’s been pretty noticeable and harder to shake off than I’d like. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is interfering with your social relationships as well?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve had any big arguments or anything like that. I’ve maybe been a little less present with people, but I don’t know if I’d call it interfering exactly.\n\nI guess I haven’t really looked at it that way. Is that something I should be paying more attention to?\n\nDoctor: Would you say there are any times of the day when your low mood is noticeably worse?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern, honestly. It kind of… comes and goes, or maybe I’m just not tracking it closely enough.\n\nI wouldn’t want to say mornings or evenings if I’m not sure. Should I be paying more attention to that?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I mean… some things, kind of? But not like before.\n\nMy guitar’s just been sitting in the corner, which feels stupid to say because I used to really like playing. And I’ve been bailing on soccer and the shelter more than I’d like to admit. Even stuff I usually enjoy, like cooking or going hiking, I can still do it sometimes, but it just feels like I’m going through the motions… like there’s not much point to it.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but yeah, I guess I’m not really getting the same pleasure out of things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thanks for seeing me. I guess I’m here because lately I’m just not thinking as clearly as I should, especially at work. It’s probably not a huge thing, but I’ll lose track of what a client is saying, or I’ll reread the same chart note a bunch of times and still not really take it in. And then I kind of freeze on simple decisions I used to handle without thinking. I’m not sure if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. Maybe a little, I guess? I don’t want to overstate it. I wouldn’t say I’m, like, completely falling apart or anything. I just feel kind of… lower than usual sometimes. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you normally like to do?\n\nPatient: Not as much, no. I mean, I still try, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but… my guitar’s just been sitting in the corner, and I keep backing out of soccer and the shelter shifts. Even cooking or going for a hike, which usually helps, feels kind of like I’m just doing it because I should, not because I actually want to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about the future?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not hopeless, exactly. I mean, I get discouraged, but I’m not sitting there thinking there’s no future or anything. Maybe I just feel a bit unsure about things right now. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is interfering with your social relationships as well?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve had any big arguments or anything like that. I’ve maybe been a little less present with people, but I don’t know if I’d call it interfering exactly.\n\nI guess I haven’t really looked at it that way. Is that something I should be paying more attention to?\n\nDoctor: Would you say there are any times of the day when your low mood is noticeably worse?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern, honestly. It kind of… comes and goes, or maybe I’m just not tracking it closely enough.\n\nI wouldn’t want to say mornings or evenings if I’m not sure. Should I be paying more attention to that?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I mean… some things, kind of? But not like before.\n\nMy guitar’s just been sitting in the corner, which feels stupid to say because I used to really like playing. And I’ve been bailing on soccer and the shelter more than I’d like to admit. Even stuff I usually enjoy, like cooking or going hiking, I can still do it sometimes, but it just feels like I’m going through the motions… like there’s not much point to it.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but yeah, I guess I’m not really getting the same pleasure out of things.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that still bring you a sense of pleasure or enjoyment?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… but honestly not much in the way it used to.\n\nLike, I can still make myself cook sometimes, or go for a hike, and I guess there are moments that aren’t terrible. But it feels more like I’m just doing the thing because I should, not because I’m really enjoying it. My guitar’s still just sitting there, and I keep skipping soccer and the shelter, which I feel bad about.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it right, but… yeah, it mostly feels kind of flat.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thanks for seeing me. I guess I’m here because lately I’m just not thinking as clearly as I should, especially at work. It’s probably not a huge thing, but I’ll lose track of what a client is saying, or I’ll reread the same chart note a bunch of times and still not really take it in. And then I kind of freeze on simple decisions I used to handle without thinking. I’m not sure if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. Maybe a little, I guess? I don’t want to overstate it. I wouldn’t say I’m, like, completely falling apart or anything. I just feel kind of… lower than usual sometimes. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you normally like to do?\n\nPatient: Not as much, no. I mean, I still try, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but… my guitar’s just been sitting in the corner, and I keep backing out of soccer and the shelter shifts. Even cooking or going for a hike, which usually helps, feels kind of like I’m just doing it because I should, not because I actually want to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about the future?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not hopeless, exactly. I mean, I get discouraged, but I’m not sitting there thinking there’s no future or anything. Maybe I just feel a bit unsure about things right now. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s been about a month or so. It kind of started after the clinic lost some staff and my schedule shifted more into late-evening coverage, plus a lot more crisis paperwork. I don’t know if that’s, like, the reason exactly, but that’s when I started noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is present most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to measure that. It’s… there a lot, I guess, but I can still have moments where I’m distracted or things feel kind of normal. So I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say every minute of the day. But yeah, it does seem to be hanging around more often than I’d like. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns recently?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, a little, I guess. Mostly appetite. I don’t really feel hungry most days unless someone kind of reminds me to eat, and then I realize I haven’t had much. I don’t mean to skip meals, it just sort of happens. My pants have been feeling looser too, so… I guess it’s probably noticeable. Is that the kind of change you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is significantly interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’ve been trying to handle it and not make it a big thing, but it got to the point where I told my supervisor I needed help. I was making mistakes and hesitating with clients, and I started worrying it could affect their care.\n\nI don’t want to sound dramatic, but I didn’t really trust myself to keep working safely without getting evaluated. Is that… is that the kind of interference you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your low mood as mild, or is it more pronounced?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it mild, exactly. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is interfering with your social relationships as well?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve had any big arguments or anything like that. I’ve maybe been a little less present with people, but I don’t know if I’d call it interfering exactly.\n\nI guess I haven’t really looked at it that way. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thanks for seeing me. I guess I’m here because lately I’m just not thinking as clearly as I should, especially at work. It’s probably not a huge thing, but I’ll lose track of what a client is saying, or I’ll reread the same chart note a bunch of times and still not really take it in. And then I kind of freeze on simple decisions I used to handle without thinking. I’m not sure if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. Maybe a little, I guess? I don’t want to overstate it. I wouldn’t say I’m, like, completely falling apart or anything. I just feel kind of… lower than usual sometimes. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you normally like to do?\n\nPatient: Not as much, no. I mean, I still try, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but… my guitar’s just been sitting in the corner, and I keep backing out of soccer and the shelter shifts. Even cooking or going for a hike, which usually helps, feels kind of like I’m just doing it because I should, not because I actually want to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about the future?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not hopeless, exactly. I mean, I get discouraged, but I’m not sitting there thinking there’s no future or anything. Maybe I just feel a bit unsure about things right now. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s been about a month or so. It kind of started after the clinic lost some staff and my schedule shifted more into late-evening coverage, plus a lot more crisis paperwork. I don’t know if that’s, like, the reason exactly, but that’s when I started noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is present most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to measure that. It’s… there a lot, I guess, but I can still have moments where I’m distracted or things feel kind of normal. So I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say every minute of the day. But yeah, it does seem to be hanging around more often than I’d like. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns recently?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, a little, I guess. Mostly appetite. I don’t really feel hungry most days unless someone kind of reminds me to eat, and then I realize I haven’t had much. I don’t mean to skip meals, it just sort of happens. My pants have been feeling looser too, so… I guess it’s probably noticeable. Is that the kind of change you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is significantly interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’ve been trying to handle it and not make it a big thing, but it got to the point where I told my supervisor I needed help. I was making mistakes and hesitating with clients, and I started worrying it could affect their care.\n\nI don’t want to sound dramatic, but I didn’t really trust myself to keep working safely without getting evaluated. Is that… is that the kind of interference you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your low mood as mild, or is it more pronounced?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it mild, exactly. I mean, I’m still getting through things, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is… but it feels more than just “a little down.” It’s been pretty noticeable and harder to shake off than I’d like. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is interfering with your social relationships as well?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve had any big arguments or anything like that. I’ve maybe been a little less present with people, but I don’t know if I’d call it interfering exactly.\n\nI guess I haven’t really looked at it that way. Is that something I should be paying more attention to?\n\nDoctor: Would you say there are any times of the day when your low mood is noticeably worse?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern, honestly. It kind of… comes and goes, or maybe I’m just not tracking it closely enough.\n\nI wouldn’t want to say mornings or evenings if I’m not sure. Should I be paying more attention to that?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I mean… some things, kind of? But not like before.\n\nMy guitar’s just been sitting in the corner, which feels stupid to say because I used to really like playing. And I’ve been bailing on soccer and the shelter more than I’d like to admit. Even stuff I usually enjoy, like cooking or going hiking, I can still do it sometimes, but it just feels like I’m going through the motions… like there’s not much point to it.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but yeah, I guess I’m not really getting the same pleasure out of things.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that still bring you a sense of pleasure or enjoyment?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… but honestly not much in the way it used to.\n\nLike, I can still make myself cook sometimes, or go for a hike, and I guess there are moments that aren’t terrible. But it feels more like I’m just doing the thing because I should, not because I’m really enjoying it. My guitar’s still just sitting there, and I keep skipping soccer and the shelter, which I feel bad about.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it right, but… yeah, it mostly feels kind of flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think most of the time, probably. I don’t want to overstate it, because I can still do things, like cook or go hiking sometimes, and it’s not like I feel absolutely nothing every second.\n\nBut it’s more like… I’m just going through the motions. My guitar’s just sitting in the corner, and I keep bailing on soccer and the shelter, which I know isn’t great. Even when I try to do the “good” things, it kind of feels like there’s no point to it.\n\nDoes that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood and related symptoms are causing you significant distress or impairment in your daily life, or are they more manageable?\n\nPatient: I mean… I want to say it’s manageable, because I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or anything.\n\nBut yeah, it has started affecting things in a way that scared me a little. I actually told my supervisor I needed help because I was making mistakes and hesitating, and it was starting to affect client care. I just… didn’t really trust myself to keep working safely without being evaluated.\n\nThat feels kind of hard to admit, but I guess that counts as significant, right?\n\nDoctor: How many days a week would you say you've been feeling this low mood?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure… I haven’t been tracking it day by day.\n\nIt feels like it’s there most days, but I don’t want to give you a number and be inaccurate. Maybe more days than not? Is that specific enough, or should I try to think through the week more carefully?\n\nDoctor: How many weeks have you been experiencing this low mood?\n\nPatient: Probably… about a month? Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you normally like to do?\n\nPatient: Not as much, no. I mean, I still try, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but… my guitar’s just been sitting in the corner, and I keep backing out of soccer and the shelter shifts. Even cooking or going for a hike, which usually helps, feels kind of like I’m just doing it because I should, not because I actually want to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about the future?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not hopeless, exactly. I mean, I get discouraged, but I’m not sitting there thinking there’s no future or anything. Maybe I just feel a bit unsure about things right now. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s been about a month or so. It kind of started after the clinic lost some staff and my schedule shifted more into late-evening coverage, plus a lot more crisis paperwork. I don’t know if that’s, like, the reason exactly, but that’s when I started noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is present most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to measure that. It’s… there a lot, I guess, but I can still have moments where I’m distracted or things feel kind of normal. So I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say every minute of the day. But yeah, it does seem to be hanging around more often than I’d like. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns recently?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, a little, I guess. Mostly appetite. I don’t really feel hungry most days unless someone kind of reminds me to eat, and then I realize I haven’t had much. I don’t mean to skip meals, it just sort of happens. My pants have been feeling looser too, so… I guess it’s probably noticeable. Is that the kind of change you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is significantly interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’ve been trying to handle it and not make it a big thing, but it got to the point where I told my supervisor I needed help. I was making mistakes and hesitating with clients, and I started worrying it could affect their care.\n\nI don’t want to sound dramatic, but I didn’t really trust myself to keep working safely without getting evaluated. Is that… is that the kind of interference you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your low mood as mild, or is it more pronounced?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it mild, exactly. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is interfering with your social relationships as well?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve had any big arguments or anything like that. I’ve maybe been a little less present with people, but I don’t know if I’d call it interfering exactly.\n\nI guess I haven’t really looked at it that way. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thanks for seeing me. I guess I’m here because lately I’m just not thinking as clearly as I should, especially at work. And then I kind of freeze on simple decisions I used to handle without thinking. I’m not sure if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. Maybe a little, I guess? I don’t want to overstate it. I wouldn’t say I’m, like, completely falling apart or anything. I just feel kind of… lower than usual sometimes. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you normally like to do?\n\nPatient: Not as much, no. I mean, I still try, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but… my guitar’s just been sitting in the corner, and I keep backing out of soccer and the shelter shifts. Even cooking or going for a hike, which usually helps, feels kind of like I’m just doing it because I should, not because I actually want to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about the future?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not hopeless, exactly. I mean, I get discouraged, but I’m not sitting there thinking there’s no future or anything. Maybe I just feel a bit unsure about things right now. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s been about a month or so. It kind of started after the clinic lost some staff and my schedule shifted more into late-evening coverage, plus a lot more crisis paperwork. I don’t know if that’s, like, the reason exactly, but that’s when I started noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is present most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to measure that. It’s… there a lot, I guess, but I can still have moments where I’m distracted or things feel kind of normal. So I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say every minute of the day. But yeah, it does seem to be hanging around more often than I’d like. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns recently?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, a little, I guess. Mostly appetite. I don’t really feel hungry most days unless someone kind of reminds me to eat, and then I realize I haven’t had much. I don’t mean to skip meals, it just sort of happens. My pants have been feeling looser too, so… I guess it’s probably noticeable. Is that the kind of change you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is significantly interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’ve been trying to handle it and not make it a big thing, but it got to the point where I told my supervisor I needed help. I was making mistakes and hesitating with clients, and I started worrying it could affect their care.\n\nI don’t want to sound dramatic, but I didn’t really trust myself to keep working safely without getting evaluated. Is that… is that the kind of interference you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your low mood as mild, or is it more pronounced?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it mild, exactly. I mean, I’m still getting through things, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is… but it feels more than just “a little down.” It’s been pretty noticeable and harder to shake off than I’d like. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is interfering with your social relationships as well?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve had any big arguments or anything like that. I’ve maybe been a little less present with people, but I don’t know if I’d call it interfering exactly.\n\nI guess I haven’t really looked at it that way. Is that something I should be paying more attention to?\n\nDoctor: Would you say there are any times of the day when your low mood is noticeably worse?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern, honestly. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thanks for seeing me. I guess I’m here because lately I’m just not thinking as clearly as I should, especially at work. And then I kind of freeze on simple decisions I used to handle without thinking. I’m not sure if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. Maybe a little, I guess? I don’t want to overstate it. I wouldn’t say I’m, like, completely falling apart or anything. I just feel kind of… lower than usual sometimes. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you normally like to do?\n\nPatient: Not as much, no. I mean, I still try, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but… my guitar’s just been sitting in the corner, and I keep backing out of soccer and the shelter shifts. Even cooking or going for a hike, which usually helps, feels kind of like I’m just doing it because I should, not because I actually want to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about the future?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not hopeless, exactly. I mean, I get discouraged, but I’m not sitting there thinking there’s no future or anything. Maybe I just feel a bit unsure about things right now. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s been about a month or so. It kind of started after the clinic lost some staff and my schedule shifted more into late-evening coverage, plus a lot more crisis paperwork. I don’t know if that’s, like, the reason exactly, but that’s when I started noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is present most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to measure that. It’s… there a lot, I guess, but I can still have moments where I’m distracted or things feel kind of normal. So I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say every minute of the day. But yeah, it does seem to be hanging around more often than I’d like. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns recently?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, a little, I guess. Mostly appetite. I don’t really feel hungry most days unless someone kind of reminds me to eat, and then I realize I haven’t had much. I don’t mean to skip meals, it just sort of happens. My pants have been feeling looser too, so… I guess it’s probably noticeable. Is that the kind of change you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is significantly interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’ve been trying to handle it and not make it a big thing, but it got to the point where I told my supervisor I needed help. I was making mistakes and hesitating with clients, and I started worrying it could affect their care.\n\nI don’t want to sound dramatic, but I didn’t really trust myself to keep working safely without getting evaluated. Is that… is that the kind of interference you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your low mood as mild, or is it more pronounced?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it mild, exactly. I mean, I’m still getting through things, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is… but it feels more than just “a little down.” It’s been pretty noticeable and harder to shake off than I’d like. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is interfering with your social relationships as well?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve had any big arguments or anything like that. I’ve maybe been a little less present with people, but I don’t know if I’d call it interfering exactly.\n\nI guess I haven’t really looked at it that way. Is that something I should be paying more attention to?\n\nDoctor: Would you say there are any times of the day when your low mood is noticeably worse?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern, honestly. It kind of… comes and goes, or maybe I’m just not tracking it closely enough.\n\nI wouldn’t want to say mornings or evenings if I’m not sure. Should I be paying more attention to that?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I mean… some things, kind of? But not like before.\n\nMy guitar’s just been sitting in the corner, which feels stupid to say because I used to really like playing. And I’ve been bailing on soccer and the shelter more than I’d like to admit. Even stuff I usually enjoy, like cooking or going hiking, I can still do it sometimes, but it just feels like I’m going through the motions… like there’s not much point to it.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but yeah, I guess I’m not really getting the same pleasure out of things.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that still bring you a sense of pleasure or enjoyment?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… but honestly not much in the way it used to.\n\nLike, I can still make myself cook sometimes, or go for a hike, and I guess there are moments that aren’t terrible. But it feels more like I’m just doing the thing because I should, not because I’m really enjoying it. My guitar’s still just sitting there, and I keep skipping soccer and the shelter, which I feel bad about.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it right, but… yeah, it mostly feels kind of flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think most of the time, probably. I don’t want to overstate it, because I can still do things, like cook or go hiking sometimes, and it’s not like I feel absolutely nothing every second.\n\nBut it’s more like… I’m just going through the motions. My guitar’s just sitting in the corner, and I keep bailing on soccer and the shelter, which I know isn’t great. Even when I try to do the “good” things, it kind of feels like there’s no point to it.\n\nDoes that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood and related symptoms are causing you significant distress or impairment in your daily life, or are they more manageable?\n\nPatient: I mean… I want to say it’s manageable, because I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or anything.\n\nBut yeah, it has started affecting things in a way that scared me a little. I actually told my supervisor I needed help because I was making mistakes and hesitating, and it was starting to affect client care. I just… didn’t really trust myself to keep working safely without being evaluated.\n\nThat feels kind of hard to admit, but I guess that counts as significant, right?\n\nDoctor: How many days a week would you say you've been feeling this low mood?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure… I haven’t been tracking it day by day.\n\nIt feels like it’s there most days, but I don’t want to give you a number and be inaccurate. Maybe more days than not? Is that specific enough, or should I try to think through the week more carefully?\n\nDoctor: How many weeks have you been experiencing this low mood?\n\nPatient: Probably… about a month? I didn’t really think of it as a “thing” at first, so I’m kind of estimating.\n\nIt started around when the clinic lost some staff and my schedule got moved more into late-evening coverage, with heavier crisis paperwork. I kept telling myself it was just stress and I’d adjust, but… yeah, it’s been roughly four weeks, I think. Does that sound like the kind of timeline you mean?\n\nDoctor: How many days in a typical week would you say you're experiencing this low mood?\n\nPatient: I’d say… probably most days. I don’t have an exact count, sorry.\n\nIf I had to put a number on it, maybe five or six days in a typical week?
